# EXISTING Toon STRUCTURE — THE SPEC

<!-- Phase 1 document. THE contract of the port. Rule: after reading this
     file, an implementer must NOT need the original's source. Behavior is
     described as WHAT (inputs → outputs, verbatim strings, exact numbers),
     never HOW. Every clause is numbered S<section>.<n> and cites its
     provenance `file:line` in the original; port defs, laws and golden cases
     refer to clauses by number. A gap discovered later is an OQ entry,
     resolved by RUNNING the original on a new case (captured as a golden),
     then amended here. Clauses are never renumbered: append, or mark
     "withdrawn: see S<x>". -->

Provenance: `legacy/Toon` (`toon_rust`) at commit `f955c67015d45330935b3ae4d62178d2bb44a481` (`toon 0.2.4`), with the locked
dependencies whose behavior reaches an output (`serde_json 1.0.151`, `zmij 1.0.23`, `indexmap 2.14.0`, `clap_builder 4.6.6`) and Rust std;
extracted 2026-09-20 by five owned-section extractors (parts A–E, `docs/spec-parts/`), each checking its clauses against the pinned
oracle `./oracle/toon`; merged by the architect. The goldens are the truth: where the source, the README or the original's own
documents disagree with a golden, the golden wins and the disagreement is an S10 row.
Coverage: every file of the in-scope surface was read (per-part lists in S12); the unread and out-of-scope parts are listed in S12.

## How this document is organized

Each section S1–S12 holds the rows of the parts that own a share of it, in part order A–E. Clause numbers are globally unique because
each part owns a range:

| part | surface | clause ranges |
|---|---|---|
| A | command line, effects, I/O errors, `--stats` | S1.1–S1.199, S4.300–S4.349, S5.100–S5.129, S6.1–S6.9, S7.1–S7.19, S8.1–S8.49, S9.1–S9.49, S10.1–S10.19 |
| B | JSON text input, the two JSON writers | S2.1–S2.99, S5.50–S5.99, S6.10–S6.19, S9.50–S9.99, S10.20–S10.39 |
| C | numbers | S4.100–S4.199, S5.30–S5.49, S7.20–S7.99, S9.200–S9.219, S10.40–S10.59 |
| D | the encoder | S3.1–S3.19, S4.1–S4.99, S5.1–S5.29, S6.30–S6.39, S10.60–S10.79 |
| E | the decoder and path expansion | S2.100–S2.199, S3.20–S3.39, S4.200–S4.299, S6.40–S6.49, S9.100–S9.199, S10.80–S10.99 |

## Amendment 2026-09-22: the pin moved to `toon_rust` `694d73b`, every known bug FIXED

The owner ordered every bug found in either program fixed, never reproduced ("ANy bugs you find in EITHER
toon_rust (my project) or toon_bend MUST be properly FIXED"). Each bug this document recorded was judged
against the TOON specification v3.0.3 and the reference TypeScript implementation (v2.1.0, and v4.1 §14 for
the strict checks), fixed in `toon_rust` (`d968de1`, `a8f7e45`, `7184fe1`, `528dd48`, `0ee264c`, `694d73b`),
and the port was re-pinned to `694d73b` (PLAN §2) and re-captured (`--repin`; 486 of 1071 cases changed).
**This block amends the clauses below. Where a clause states the old behavior, this block wins.** The
clauses keep their numbers and their old text as the record of what the original did before `694d73b`.
Every example here is a captured golden of the new pin.

### A1. Superseded clauses and their new behavior

## Notation


### Notation common to every part
- **The rule.** A character or spelling that STANDS FOR something other than itself is declared in this block or
  in its part's block below. Every other character in an example stands for itself: `é`, `😀`, `你`, `١`, `ｔ`, `€`
  and the like are literal test data, and `…`, `§`, `±`, `≠`, `÷`, `←`, `·`, `⌊ ⌋` are ordinary prose and arithmetic.
  (Round 15, R15-7: the part B block below claimed its examples used only `⏎`, `⇥` and `→`, from a search for the
  symbols already known; an inventory of every non-ASCII character the clause rows use found the ones declared here.)
- `␍` stands for one raw CARRIAGE RETURN byte (U+000D) and `␀` for one raw NUL byte (U+0000), in input or output
  (S2.5, S2.40, S2.42, S9.50, S9.55, S9.57, S9.58, S9.62, …). `⏎` and `⇥` are declared in part D, `␠` in part E.
- `\xNN`, with two hex digits, stands for one raw byte of that value in INPUT (S2.2, S9.9, S9.10, S9.58, S9.62,
  S9.67). JSON has no `\x` escape, so this spelling never denotes JSON text; `\x0B1` is the byte 0x0B then `1`.
- `U+XXXX` written bare names that one code point, exactly as `<U+XXXX>` does in part D.
- `‹BOM›` stands for the single character U+FEFF, which would otherwise be invisible (S2.106, S2.114).
- `⊘` and `⊗` are ONE correctly rounded binary64 division and multiplication (S4.315, S7.6), unlike `/`, `×` and
  `·`, which are exact arithmetic on the real numbers.

### Notation, part A (command line, effects, I/O errors, --stats)
- Inside backticks `\n` is one LF byte (0x0A). A real TAB is written "U+0009"; the two-character word backslash-`t` is always called out in words. No text produced by this surface itself contains CR.
- `→` is U+2192 RIGHTWARDS ARROW, UTF-8 bytes `E2 86 92`. It is never the ASCII pair `->`.
- "stderr, exit 2" / "stderr, exit 1" always also means: stdout is empty (0 bytes) unless the clause says otherwise.
- Provenance abbreviations: `args.rs` = `legacy/Toon/src/cli/args.rs`; `mod.rs` = `legacy/Toon/src/cli/mod.rs`; `main.rs`, `error.rs`, `options.rs`, `conversion.rs` likewise under `legacy/Toon/src/`; `clap:` = `clap_builder-4.6.6/src/`; `strsim:` = `strsim-0.11.1/src/`; `anstream:` = `anstream-1.0.0/src/`. `[run]` marks a statement verified by an oracle run made for this part (listed in the Extractor report); `[inference]` marks a statement read from source and not separately observable.
- `<prog>`: the program name printed in `Usage:` lines (S1.2). All goldens have `toon`; templates below write `toon`.
- "C" and "P" are the committed list and the pending item of S1.13. They are descriptive devices for the observable precedence and `Usage:` rules, not a data-structure prescription.

### Notation, part C (numbers: four text/value algorithms and their glue)
- **binary64**: the IEEE-754 double format: sign, 11-bit exponent, 52-bit fraction. A positive finite value is
  m × 2^e with integer m < 2^53 and e ≥ −1074; it is *normal* when m ≥ 2^52 and *subnormal* when e = −1074 and
  m < 2^52. The largest finite value is (2^53 − 1) × 2^971 = `1.7976931348623157e308`; the smallest positive
  value is 2^−1074 = `5e-324`.
- **RNE** (round to nearest, ties to even): the representable binary64 value nearest to an exact real x; when x
  is exactly halfway between two neighbours, the neighbour whose m is even. Representable values below 2^−1022
  are the multiples of 2^−1074, so a result in that range is rounded ONCE, directly to that grid (gradual
  underflow); an exact x with \|x\| ≤ 2^−1075 gives zero (the halfway point 2^−1075 goes to zero because zero is
  the even neighbour). An exact x with \|x\| ≥ 2^1024 − 2^970 gives infinity.
- One **IEEE operation** means: take the two binary64 operands as exact rationals, form the exact product or
  quotient, apply RNE once. Nothing else (no extended precision, no fused operations) happens in the original.
- **S**: the unsigned 64-bit decimal significand collected from a JSON literal. **E10**: the signed decimal
  exponent adjustment collected while reading digits. **X**: the magnitude written after `e`/`E`.
  **T**: the total decimal exponent. **F0** = RNE(S). **P(k)**: the power-of-ten table entry (S4.112).

### Notation, part D (the encoder: value to TOON lines, key folding)
- `⏎` stands for the line break between two output lines (one U+000A byte in stdout). The final `\n` that the CLI
  writes after the last line (S5.1) is not shown in examples.
- `⇥` stands for one raw TAB (U+0009) in the output. `<U+XXXX>` stands for that single raw code point.
- Inside a table a literal pipe character is written `\|`; it is one `|` byte in the real text.
- Inside code spans every space is literal and counts. Indentation in examples is real.
- In OUTPUT text, a backslash sequence inside double quotes (such as `\n` or `\"`) is two literal characters
  (a backslash and a letter or quote). In JSON INPUT text the same spelling is a JSON escape.
- "depth" is a non-negative integer; a line at depth d starts with `indent × d` spaces (S4.61). Examples use the
  default indent 2 and the default delimiter comma unless flags are shown in square brackets.

### Notation, part B (JSON text input and the two JSON writers)
- Part B's examples use `⏎`, `⇥` and `→` with the meanings part D gives them, the part D rule on a backslash
  inside double quotes (two literal characters in OUTPUT, a JSON escape in JSON INPUT), and `␍`, `␀`, `\xNN` and
  `U+XXXX` as the common block above declares them. This block exists so that every part named in a section
  heading has one (round 14, R14-14). It used to say part B used ONLY `⏎`, `⇥` and `→`; round 15 (R15-7) found
  `␍` in 7 rows and `␀` in 6.

### Notation, part E (the decoder: TOON lines to events and values, path expansion)
- `␠` stands for ONE space (U+0020), spelled out wherever a leading, trailing or repeated space decides the
  result — in TOON input (`␠␠- x` is a list item at depth 1) and in the one stdout example below, whose
  indentation it makes countable. Elsewhere a space in a code span is literal, as in part D.
- `⏎` is the line break between two INPUT lines here (one U+000A byte), as part D uses it for output lines.
- **The right side of `input → value` is the DECODED VALUE of the construct under discussion, written as
  compact JSON — it is NOT the program's stdout.** The real stdout wraps that value in the document's root object
  and lays it out with JSON writer A (S5.50–S5.99), numbers printed as binary64 (`1` → `1.0`). Example, S4.226:
  `items[3]:⏎␠␠- 1⏎␠␠- a: 1⏎␠␠- text` → `[1.0,{"a":1.0},"text"]` names the value of `items`; the pinned original
  prints `{⏎␠␠"items": [⏎␠␠␠␠1.0,⏎␠␠␠␠{⏎␠␠␠␠␠␠"a": 1.0⏎␠␠␠␠},⏎␠␠␠␠"text"⏎␠␠]⏎}` (run on 2026-09-22). To predict
  stdout from a part E clause, apply writer A to the value shown.
- `→ ERR: <message>` means decoding FAILS with exactly that message on stderr and exit code 1 (S9, part E).
- "probes:" after the main example lists further `input → value` pairs for the same clause, in the same notation.


## S1. Command-line surface

### S1, part A (command line, effects, I/O errors, --stats): Command-line surface

| S1.n | clause | provenance | cases |
|---|---|---|---|
| S1.1 | Invocation form: `toon [OPTIONS] [INPUT]`. At most one positional word INPUT, the ten options of table T1, plus `-h`/`--help` and `-V`/`--version`. Options and INPUT may come in any order (options after INPUT are honored). There are no subcommands (the word `help` is an ordinary INPUT path: `toon help` → S9.4 with path `help`), no abbreviation of long names, no response files, no configuration file. | args.rs:5-59 | usage_help, flag_output_file_label, usage_word_help_is_a_path |
| S1.2 | `<prog>` in every `Usage:` line (help text and error texts) is the final path component of argv[0] exactly as given (no extension stripping, spaces kept): run as `./oracle/toon` it is `toon`; run through a link named `renamed-tool` it is `renamed-tool`; argv[0] = `/a/b/c.d` gives `c.d` [run]. The version line (S1.62) and the first line of the help text never depend on argv[0]. The whole corpus is captured with `<prog>` = `toon`. | clap:builder/command.rs:902; args.rs:6 | usage_unknown_flag, usage_conflict_encode_decode, usage_help |
| S1.3 | The options, their spellings, value names, defaults and consumers are exactly table T1 below. Long names are matched exactly and case-sensitively; the only short letters are `o`, `e`, `d`, `h`, `V` (case-sensitive: `-E`, `-D`, `-H`, `-v` are unknown). | args.rs:16-58 | usage_help, usage_unknown_short, usage_unknown_short_uppercase |
| S1.4 | argv words after argv[0] are examined strictly left to right and the first error *reported* (S1.15) ends the run; later words are never examined (they may be arbitrarily malformed). No input is read, and no file is opened or created, before the whole argv has been accepted (S1.135). | clap:parser/parser.rs:77; mod.rs:19-21 | usage_help_wins_over_bad_flag_value, usage_extra_positional, usage_help_first_wins |
| S1.5 | Word classes, while no escape has been seen: (a) exactly `--`: the escape (S1.10); (b) begins with `--` and is longer than two characters: a long-option word (S1.6); (c) begins with `-`, has at least two characters, second character not `-`: a short cluster (S1.8); (d) every other word, including the one-character word `-` and the empty word: a plain word. After the escape every word is a plain word and can only be a positional. | clap:parser/parser.rs:131-213; clap_lex-1.1.0/src/lib.rs:308-360 | flag_stdin_dash, flag_double_dash_file, usage_empty_arg, usage_unknown_short |
| S1.6 | A long-option word is split at its first `=` into name and attached value (no `=`: no attached value; `--name=`: attached empty value; `--indent=4=5`: value `4=5`). The name must equal one of the twelve long names; otherwise the word is an unknown long option (S1.71, or S1.73 when a similar name exists) and the message shows `--` + name without the `=value` part (`--bogus=1` reports `--bogus`; `--=x` reports `--`; `---x` reports `---x`). | clap:parser/parser.rs:763-866; clap_lex-1.1.0/src/lib.rs:324-339 | usage_unknown_flag, flag_indent_equals, usage_unknown_long_with_value, usage_unknown_long_empty_name, usage_unknown_triple_dash |
| S1.7 | A value-taking option written `--opt=value` takes `value` verbatim: it may be empty, begin with `-` or `--`, or contain `=` (`--indent=-1`, `--output=--weird`, `--delimiter=--`). Written without `=`, the option waits for the next word, and only a plain word (class d, which includes `-`) becomes its value. A following escape, long-option word or short cluster is never taken as the value: `--indent -1`, `--indent -0`, `-o -x` fail on the second word as an unknown short character (S1.77: `-1`, `-0`, `-x`), `--output --weird` fails as an unknown long option, and `--indent --encode` / `--indent -e` fail with the missing-value error S1.81. | clap:parser/parser.rs:291-315, 1018-1080 | usage_bad_indent_negative, usage_bad_flatten_depth_neg, flag_indent_equals, flag_delimiter_equals_pipe, flag_output_equals, usage_negative_number_word, usage_output_value_flaglike, usage_output_value_longlike, usage_missing_value_before_flag |
| S1.8 | Short cluster: its characters after the leading `-` are processed left to right. `e`, `d`, `h`, `V` act exactly as `--encode`, `--decode`, `--help`, `--version`. `o` is `--output` and ends the cluster: the rest of the word, after removing one leading `=` when present, is its attached value when that rest is non-empty (`-oFILE`, `-o=FILE`, `-eoFILE`; `-oe` writes a file named `e`); when nothing follows `o` the option waits for the next word as in S1.7 (`-o FILE`, `-eo FILE`, `-do FILE`); `-o=` is an attached empty value (S1.82). Any other character c stops the run with S1.77 naming `-c`, the first unknown character only (`-ex` → `-x`, `-e5` → `-5`, `-129` → `-1`, `-e=x` → `-=`); flags earlier in the same cluster have already been accepted (S1.15), so `-eh` prints help, `-ed` is the conflict S1.79, `-ee` is S1.80, `-hx` prints help and `-xh` is S1.77. | clap:parser/parser.rs:882-1016 | flag_short_combined_e, flag_short_d, flag_output_attached, usage_conflict_encode_decode, usage_encode_twice, usage_unknown_short, flag_cluster_eo, flag_cluster_eo_attached, flag_output_short_equals, usage_cluster_conflict, usage_cluster_unknown_after_flag, usage_cluster_equals, usage_cluster_hV, usage_cluster_Vh |
| S1.9 | A plain word that is not consumed as an option value is INPUT when no INPUT has been seen yet; otherwise it is an extra positional (S1.78). INPUT is any non-empty word; the empty word gives S1.82 naming `[INPUT]`. A plain word following an option whose single value is already collected is INPUT (`--indent 4 5` reads the file `5`). | clap:parser/parser.rs:400-430; args.rs:16-18 | usage_extra_positional, usage_empty_arg, auto_json_ext, usage_value_then_positional, usage_three_positionals |

Every later word is positional even when it begins with `-`: `-- --help` reads a file named `--help` (S9.4), `-e -- -d` reads a file named `-d`, `-- -` and `--` alone read stdin. A second positional after the escape gives S1.78 without any tip (`-- a --bogus` reports `--bogus` with no tip). | S1.13 | Parsing state used by S1.14–S1.19 and by every context-built `Usage:` line: C, the committed list, an ordered list of distinct arguments (options, flags, INPUT) accepted so far, initially empty; and at most one pending item P, which is either a value-taking option met without an attached value (holding zero or one collected value word) or the INPUT word. Flags and options with an attached value never become pending. | clap:parser/parser.rs:1119-1135; clap:parser/arg_matcher.rs:1 | usage_help_wins_over_bad_flag_value, usage_indent_twice, usage_unknown_long_swallows_bad_value |
| S1.14 | Resolving P performs these steps in order and stops at the first failure: (1) P is an option with no collected value → missing-value error S1.81 (nothing is added to C); (2) P's option is already in C → used-multiple-times error S1.80, and that option is removed from C while the others keep their order; (3) P's argument is appended to C; (4) P's value is validated by S1.30–S1.34 → error S1.82/S1.83/S1.84 (the argument stays in C). Afterwards there is no pending item. INPUT as P uses steps 3 and 4 only. | clap:parser/parser.rs:1141-1213, 1335-1356 | usage_indent_twice, usage_missing_value_indent, usage_bad_indent_17, usage_twice_beats_bad_value, usage_missing_beats_twice, usage_twice_swallowed_removes |
| S1.15 | Handling of each word: (i) known flag (long form, or a flag character of a cluster): resolve P reporting its error; then steps 2 and 3 of S1.14 for the flag. (ii) known value option with attached value: resolve P reporting its error; then steps 2, 3, 4 for the new option at once. (iii) known value option without attached value: resolve P reporting its error; the option becomes P with no value. (iv) plain word while P is an option with no value and no escape seen: the word becomes P's value; nothing is validated yet. (v) plain word otherwise, INPUT not yet seen: resolve P reporting its error; the word becomes P as INPUT. (vi) unknown long name, unknown short character, or extra positional: resolve P *discarding* its error but keeping its effect on C, then report S1.71/S1.73/S1.77/S1.78. (vii) known flag written with `=value`: report S1.86 at once without resolving P. (viii) the escape: nothing is resolved. | clap:parser/parser.rs:179-198, 263-271, 412, 474, 994, 1077, 1150 | usage_help_wins_over_bad_flag_value, usage_unknown_flag, usage_extra_positional, usage_unknown_long_swallows_bad_value, usage_unknown_short_swallows_bad_value, usage_extra_positional_swallows_bad_value, usage_flag_with_value_after_pending |
| S1.16 | At the end of argv: resolve P reporting its error; then the conflict rule S1.18; then absent options take the defaults of T1 and the program runs in the mode of S4.300. | clap:parser/parser.rs:49-72 | usage_missing_value_indent, usage_bad_indent_17, flag_short_combined_e |
| S1.17 | Every option and flag may occur at most once, all spellings counted together (`-e --encode`, `-e -e`, `-ee`, `-o a -o b`, `--indent 2 --indent 4`, `--stats --stats`): the second occurrence gives S1.80. | S1.19 | `--help`/`-h` and `--version`/`-V` act at the moment the scan reaches them: P is resolved first (its error is reported and wins: `--indent 99 --help` → S1.83; `"" --help` → S1.82 for `[INPUT]`), then the text of S1.60 or S1.62 goes to stdout and the process exits 0. Everything to their right is ignored (`--help --indent 99`, `--help --bogus`, `-V --bogus`, `-hx` succeed); whichever of help/version comes first wins (`--version --help` prints the version, `-hV` help, `-Vh` version). Flags already committed do not matter, not even conflicting ones (`-e -d --help` prints help, `-e -d --version` the version) but an earlier reported error does (`-e -e --help` → S1.80; `--bogus --help` → S1.71; `a b --help` → S1.78). | clap:parser/parser.rs:1302-1331 | usage_help, usage_help_short, usage_version, usage_version_short, usage_help_wins_over_bad_flag_value, usage_help_first_wins, usage_version_first_wins, usage_help_before_version, usage_conflict_beaten_by_help, usage_empty_input_beats_help, usage_input_then_help |
| S1.20 | Exit codes of the whole program: 0 = successful conversion, help, version; 1 = every runtime failure (S9.1–S9.19); 2 = every argv error (S1.70–S1.87). With stdout/stderr not a terminal and `CLICOLOR_FORCE` unset (the corpus condition) every text is plain. The version line and all runtime messages (S5.105–S5.111, S9) are never styled. `COLUMNS` and terminal width have no effect (help is never wrapped) [run]. | Cargo.toml:37; anstream:auto.rs:198-222; anstyle-query-1.1.5/src/lib.rs:23-68 | usage_help, usage_unknown_flag, usage_version |

**T1 — options (reference table for S1.3; not clauses).** "consumer" says which mode reads the value; every option is parsed and validated in both modes (S1.150).

| long | short | takes a value | value name | default | accepted values | consumer |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `--output` | `-o` | yes | `FILE` | absent: write stdout | any non-empty word (S1.34) | both |
| `--encode` | `-e` | no | | | | mode choice (S4.300) |
| `--decode` | `-d` | no | | | | mode choice (S4.300) |
| `--delimiter` | | yes | `DELIMITER` | `,` | S1.30 | encode |
| `--indent` | | yes | `INDENT` | `2` | S1.31 | both (S1.151) |
| `--no-strict` | | no | | strict | | decode |
| `--key-folding` | | yes | `KEY_FOLDING` | `off` | `off`, `safe` (S1.33) | encode |
| `--flatten-depth` | | yes | `N` | absent: unlimited | S1.32 | encode, only with `--key-folding safe` |
| `--expand-paths` | | yes | `EXPAND_PATHS` | `off` | `off`, `safe` (S1.33) | decode |
| `--stats` | | no | | | | encode |
| `--help` | `-h` | no | | | | S1.19 |
| `--version` | `-V` | no | | | | S1.19 |

**T2 — display form of each argument in messages and context-built `Usage:` lines (reference for S1.70–S1.87).** The long form is always shown, whatever spelling was typed (`-e -e` reports `--encode`; `-o` reports `--output <FILE>`).

| argument | in messages | in a context-built `Usage:` line |
|---|---|---|
| INPUT | `[INPUT]` | `<INPUT>` when INPUT is in C, else `[INPUT]`; always last |
| output | `--output <FILE>` | `--output <FILE>` |
| encode / decode / no-strict / stats / help / version | `--encode` / `--decode` / `--no-strict` / `--stats` / `--help` / `--version` | same |
| delimiter | `--delimiter <DELIMITER>` | same |
| indent | `--indent <INDENT>` | same |
| key-folding | `--key-folding <KEY_FOLDING>` | same |
| flatten-depth | `--flatten-depth <N>` | same |
| expand-paths | `--expand-paths <EXPAND_PATHS>` | same |

Every other word gives S1.83 with reason R-DELIM (S1.83): the empty word, `x`, `invalid`, `,,`, `TAB`, `Comma`, a space, `;`, backslash-`n`, backslash-backslash-`t`, backslash-pipe, `-`, `--`, `é`. | args.rs:73-82, 32-34 | flag_delimiter_equals_pipe, flag_delimiter_word_comma, flag_delimiter_word_pipe, flag_delimiter_word_tab, flag_delimiter_backslash_t, flag_delimiter_real_tab, usage_bad_delimiter, usage_bad_delimiter_word, usage_bad_delimiter_empty, usage_bad_delimiter_two_chars, usage_delimiter_upper, usage_delimiter_backslash_pipe |
| S1.31 | `--indent` value grammar: an optional single `+` or `-`, then one or more ASCII digits `0`–`9`, nothing else (no spaces, `.`, `e`, `0x`, `_`, non-ASCII digits). The digits are read as a signed 64-bit integer (leading zeros allowed) and the result must lie in 0..=16. Accepted examples: `0`, `16`, `+4`, `04`, `000`, `0016`, `+16`, and `-0` (writable only as `--indent=-0`, S1.7) [run]. Failure reasons, first that applies: empty word → `cannot parse integer from empty string`; grammar violation, including a lone `+` or `-`, `++4`, `4+`, ` 4`, `4 `, `2.5`, `1e1`, `0x10`, `١` → `invalid digit found in string`; above 9223372036854775807 → `number too large to fit in target type`; below −9223372036854775808 → `number too small to fit in target type`; otherwise outside 0..=16 → `<n> is not in 0..=16` with `<n>` the parsed integer in canonical decimal (`+017` → `17`, `099` → `99`, `-007` → `-7`, `256` → `256`, `9223372036854775807` → itself). All are S1.83. | args.rs:36-38; clap:builder/value_parser.rs:1406-1455 | flag_indent_0_encode, flag_indent_16_encode, flag_indent_1_encode, flag_indent_equals, usage_indent_plus, usage_indent_leading_zero, usage_bad_indent_17, usage_bad_indent_99, usage_bad_indent_256, usage_bad_indent_huge, usage_bad_indent_abc, usage_bad_indent_empty, usage_bad_indent_float, usage_bad_indent_negative, usage_indent_i64_max, usage_indent_i64_overflow, usage_indent_i64_underflow, usage_indent_canonical_echo, usage_indent_space, usage_indent_arabic_digit, usage_indent_minus_zero, flag_indent_plus_nested |
| S1.32 | `--flatten-depth` value grammar: an optional single `+`, then one or more ASCII digits; read as an unsigned 64-bit integer, leading zeros allowed (`007`, `+3`); every value 0..=18446744073709551615 is accepted. Failure reasons: empty word → `cannot parse integer from empty string`; any other character, a leading `-` (reachable in two spellings: ATTACHED with `=` whatever follows — `--flatten-depth=-`, `=-0`, `=-5`, `=--1`, `=-x` — and the lone SEPARATE word `-`. A separate word of `-` followed by anything — `-0`, `-5`, `--1`, `-x` — is not a value but an unexpected argument, S1.5c and S1.77; following that error's own tip, `--flatten-depth -- -0`, does not reach this parser either, because `--` ends the options and the flag is left without a value: `a value is required for '--flatten-depth <N>' but none was supplied`. Round 14's R14-15 amendment said `=-0` was the ONLY spelling, from two runs; round 15 (R15-8) found two more, and this text is what twelve spellings run on the original give, ten of them also on the port and all ten byte-identical), a lone `+` → `invalid digit found in string`; above 18446744073709551615 → `number too large to fit in target type`. All are S1.83. Block V1 (`goldens/usage_help.out`, byte for byte; the block's last line ends with LF and nothing follows it):

```text
TOON reference implementation in Rust (JSON <-> TOON)

Usage: toon [OPTIONS] [INPUT]

Arguments:
  [INPUT]  Input file path (omit or use "-" to read from stdin)

Options:
  -o, --output <FILE>                Output file path (stdout if omitted)
  -e, --encode                       Encode JSON to TOON (auto-detected by default)
  -d, --decode                       Decode TOON to JSON (auto-detected by default)
      --delimiter <DELIMITER>        Delimiter for arrays: comma (,), tab (\t), or pipe (|) [default: ,]
      --indent <INDENT>              Indentation size (spaces) [default: 2]
      --no-strict                    Disable strict mode for decoding (allows lenient parsing)
      --key-folding <KEY_FOLDING>    Key folding mode: off or safe [default: off] [possible values: off, safe]
      --flatten-depth <N>            Maximum folded segment count when key folding is enabled
      --expand-paths <EXPAND_PATHS>  Path expansion mode: off or safe (decode only) [default: off] [possible values: off, safe]
      --stats                        Show token statistics (encode only)
  -h, --help                         Print help
  -V, --version                      Print version

EXAMPLES:
    toon input.json                  # Encode JSON to TOON (stdout)
    toon input.toon                  # Decode TOON to JSON (stdout)
    toon input.json -o output.toon   # Encode to file
    cat data.json | toon --encode    # Encode from stdin
    cat data.toon | toon --decode    # Decode from stdin
    toon input.json --stats          # Show token statistics
```

In V1 the `(\t)` on the `--delimiter` line is the two characters backslash and `t`, not a TAB; option lines start with two spaces, long-only options with six; the description column starts at column 38 (1-based) on every option line.

#### S1 (continued): argv error shapes

Every shape below is given as a template with `<placeholders>` and one worked example copied from a golden (or from an oracle run where the corpus has none). Blank lines are real empty lines (`\n\n`); every text ends with exactly one LF after the final `.`.

| S1.n | clause | provenance | cases |
|---|---|---|---|
| S1.70 | Common frame of every argv error: the text goes to stderr, stdout stays empty, exit code 2, nothing was read from stdin or any file and no output file was created or truncated. The first line starts with `error: `; the last line is `For more information, try '--help'.` preceded by one empty line and followed by one LF. No help text, no second error, no ANSI escapes (S1.21). Values and words are echoed raw between single quotes with no escaping whatsoever (a quote, TAB or LF inside the word is copied as is) [run]. A failure to write the text is ignored (exit still 2). | clap:error/format.rs:66-130, 457-467; clap:error/mod.rs:222-248 | usage_unknown_flag, usage_bad_indent_17, usage_conflict_encode_decode, usage_delimiter_newline |
| S1.71 | Unknown long option without a similar name (S1.75 finds no candidate). Template: `error: unexpected argument '--<name>' found\n\n  tip: to pass '--<name>' as a value, use '-- --<name>'\n\n<usage>\n\nFor more information, try '--help'.\n` where `<usage>` is the smart usage line S1.72 built from C after the discard-resolve of S1.15(vi). The tip line starts with two spaces. Worked example (argv `--no-such-flag`, 187 bytes): `error: unexpected argument '--no-such-flag' found\n\n  tip: to pass '--no-such-flag' as a value, use '-- --no-such-flag'\n\nUsage: toon [OPTIONS] [INPUT]\n\nFor more information, try '--help'.\n`. | clap:parser/parser.rs:1555-1620; clap:error/mod.rs:717-760; clap:error/format.rs:116, 358 | usage_unknown_flag, usage_unknown_long_beats_conflict, usage_unknown_long_uppercase |
| S1.72 | Smart usage line: when C is empty it is the fixed `Usage: toon [OPTIONS] [INPUT]`. Otherwise it is `Usage: toon ` + the T2 display forms of the options and flags in C, in C's order, each followed by one space + `<INPUT>` when INPUT is in C, else `[INPUT]` (no `[OPTIONS]` word). Examples [run]: `-e -d --bogus` → `Usage: toon --encode --decode [INPUT]`; `--indent 99 --bogus` → `Usage: toon --indent <INDENT> [INPUT]` (step 3 of S1.14 happened before step 4 failed); `--indent --bogus` → `Usage: toon [OPTIONS] [INPUT]` (step 1 failed, nothing committed); `a.json --bogus` and `"" --bogus` → `Usage: toon <INPUT>`; `-e -d --indent 99 --bogus` → `Usage: toon --encode --decode --indent <INDENT> [INPUT]`; `--indent 2 -e --stats --indent 4 --bogus` → `Usage: toon --encode --stats [INPUT]` (step 2 removed `--indent`). | clap:output/usage.rs:75-168, 254-376; clap:parser/parser.rs:1586-1595 | usage_unknown_flag, usage_unknown_long_beats_conflict, usage_unknown_long_swallows_bad_value, usage_missing_value_swallowed, usage_unknown_long_after_input, usage_twice_swallowed_removes |
| S1.73 | Unknown long option with a similar name (in scope: PLAN §3's exclusion of this tip was withdrawn in Phase 1, OQ-A1). Template: `error: unexpected argument '--<name>' found\n\n  tip: a similar argument exists: '--<candidate>'\n\n<usage>\n\nFor more information, try '--help'.\n`: the `--` tip of S1.71 is replaced, and `<usage>` is S1.72 built from C with `<candidate>` appended when it is not already in C. Worked examples [run]: `--encod` → `error: unexpected argument '--encod' found\n\n  tip: a similar argument exists: '--encode'\n\nUsage: toon --encode [INPUT]\n\nFor more information, try '--help'.\n`; `--e` → candidate `--help`, `Usage: toon --help [INPUT]`; `--nope` → candidate `--encode`; `--stats -e plain --nope` → `Usage: toon --stats --encode <INPUT>`; `--Encode` → candidate `--encode`; `--HELP`, `--x`, `--bogus`, `--no-such-flag` have no candidate. A model built from S1.73–S1.75 reproduced 566 of 566 oracle runs over 226 generated names with four different C prefixes. | clap:parser/parser.rs:1555-1620; clap:parser/features/suggestions.rs:8-37 | usage_unknown_flag, usage_similar_arg, usage_similar_arg_help, usage_similar_arg_nope, usage_similar_arg_after_commit |
| S1.74 | Similarity J(a, b) of two strings taken as sequences of Unicode scalar values, lengths \|a\| and \|b\|: J = 0 when either is empty. Let w = max(⌊max(\|a\|, \|b\|) / 2⌋ − 1, 0). Going through a left to right, a[i] is matched with the leftmost not yet matched position j of b such that \|i − j\| ≤ w and b[j] = a[i], when one exists. m = number of matched characters; J = 0 when m = 0. h = number of ranks k at which the k-th matched character of a (in a's order) differs from the k-th matched character of b (in b's order); t = ⌊h / 2⌋. J = ((m/\|a\| + m/\|b\|) + (m − t)/m) / 3, evaluated in binary64 in that order. "Similar" means J > 0.7 with 0.7 the binary64 literal (0.6999999999999999556). Matching is case-sensitive. | strsim:lib.rs:90-164; clap:parser/features/suggestions.rs:19-21 | usage_bad_key_folding_case, usage_bad_key_folding, usage_enum_similar_boundary_30, usage_enum_similar_boundary_31 |
| S1.75 | Candidate lists, in order: for an unknown long name: `output`, `encode`, `decode`, `delimiter`, `indent`, `no-strict`, `key-folding`, `flatten-depth`, `expand-paths`, `stats`, `help`, `version`; for a `--key-folding`/`--expand-paths` value: `off`, `safe`. Among the candidates with J > 0.7 the one with the greatest J is chosen; when several share the greatest J (equal binary64 values) the one *later* in the list wins [run: `--d` → `indent` over `decode`; `--c` → `decode` over `encode`; value `ofae` → `safe` over `off`, both 13/18]. With no candidate above 0.7 there is no similarity tip. The name compared is the text between `--` and the first `=`. | clap:parser/features/suggestions.rs:15-36, 56-58; clap:error/mod.rs:454-482 | usage_bad_key_folding, usage_unknown_flag, usage_enum_similar_tie, usage_similar_arg_tie |
| S1.76 | Reference values of J [run, model = oracle on 46 of 46 values]: J(`Safe`,`safe`) = 0.8333 → tip `safe`; J(`saf`,`safe`) = 0.9167; J(`of`,`off`) = 0.8889 → tip `off`; J(`on`,`off`) = 0.6111 and J(`on`,`safe`) = 0 → no tip; J(`sofa`,`off`) = 0.7222 > J(`sofa`,`safe`) = 0.6667 → tip `off`; J(`SAFE`,`safe`) = 0 and J(`maybe`, either) ≤ 0.7 → no tip (goldens); `off` followed by 27 `x` (30 characters): exact value 7/10, binary64 result 0.70000000000000007 > 0.7 → tip `off`; with 28 `x`: 0.6989 → no tip; `safe` followed by 36 `x` (40 characters) → tip `safe`; with 37 `x` → no tip. | strsim:lib.rs:156-163 | usage_bad_key_folding, usage_bad_key_folding_case, usage_bad_expand_paths, usage_enum_similar_case, usage_enum_similar_picks_best, usage_enum_not_similar |

Template: `error: unexpected argument '<word>' found\n\nUsage: toon [OPTIONS] [INPUT]\n\nFor more information, try '--help'.\n`: no tip line ever (also after the escape, also when the word begins with `-`), fixed `Usage:` line. #### S1 (continued): which error wins

| S1.n | clause | provenance | cases |
|---|---|---|---|
| S1.130 | With several problems in one argv, exactly one text is printed: the one S1.15 *reports* first while scanning left to right; a conflict (S1.18) is considered only when the scan ends without any report. Help and version count as reports. Examples [run]: `--indent 99 --delimiter x` → invalid `99`; `--delimiter x --indent 99` → invalid `x`; `--bogus -e -d` → unknown `--bogus`; `a.json b.json --indent 99` → extra `b.json`; `--bogus --help` → unknown `--bogus`; `--help --bogus` → help. | clap:parser/parser.rs:77-505 | usage_help_wins_over_bad_flag_value, usage_help_first_wins, usage_unknown_long_first |
| S1.131 | A value given as a separate word is validated late, when P is resolved by the next reporting event or at the end of argv; an attached value is validated at once. Consequences: `--indent 99 --help` → S1.83 for `99` (this is the golden whose name says "help wins": it does not); `--indent 99 -V` → S1.83; `--help --indent 99` → help; `--indent=99 --indent=2` → S1.83 for `99`; `--indent=2 --indent=99` → S1.80. | clap:parser/parser.rs:1077, 1150 | usage_help_wins_over_bad_flag_value, usage_help_first_wins, usage_attached_bad_value_first, usage_attached_twice |
| S1.132 | The discard rule of S1.15(vi): an unknown long name, an unknown short character or an extra positional that *immediately follows* a pending item hides that item's own error. `--indent 99 --bogus` → S1.71 (with `Usage: toon --indent <INDENT> [INPUT]`); `--indent 99 -x` → S1.77; `a.json --indent 99 b.json` → S1.78 for `b.json`; `--indent --bogus` → S1.71 with the fixed usage line; `--indent 2 --indent 4 --bogus` → S1.71 with the fixed usage line. But a word that resolves P with reporting keeps the item's error in front: `--indent 99 a.json b.json` → S1.83 for `99` (the INPUT word `a.json` reported it); `--delimiter x a b` → S1.83 for `x`. | clap:parser/parser.rs:187, 271, 412, 474 | usage_extra_positional, usage_unknown_long_swallows_bad_value, usage_unknown_short_swallows_bad_value, usage_extra_positional_swallows_bad_value, usage_bad_value_before_extra_positional, usage_missing_value_swallowed |
| S1.133 | The encode/decode conflict loses to everything that is reported during the scan, wherever it sits: `-e -d --indent 99` and `--indent 99 -e -d` → S1.83; `-e -d --bogus` → S1.71 (usage line shows both flags); `-e -d a.json b.json` → S1.78; `-e -d --indent` → S1.81; `-e -d --help` → help, exit 0; `-e -d --version` → version, exit 0; `-e -e -d`, `-e -d -e`, `-d -e -e` → S1.80 for `--encode`. | clap:parser/parser.rs:49-72; clap:parser/validator.rs:24-60 | usage_conflict_encode_decode, usage_encode_twice, usage_bad_value_beats_conflict, usage_unknown_long_beats_conflict, usage_conflict_beaten_by_help |
| S1.134 | Within one option the order is S1.14: missing value, then used-multiple-times, then value validation. `--indent 2 --indent` → S1.81; `--indent 2 --indent 99` → S1.80 (not the range error); `-e -e --indent 99` → S1.80 for `--encode` (leftmost report). | clap:parser/parser.rs:1158-1217 | usage_indent_twice, usage_missing_beats_twice, usage_twice_beats_bad_value |
| S1.135 | Every argv error precedes every effect of S8: a conflict, a bad value or an unknown word next to a missing/unreadable INPUT, an uncreatable `-o` path, or invalid stdin content still gives the argv error with exit 2; stdin is not read (the corpus cases with a stdin fixture and exit 2 never consume it). Conversely a well-formed argv with a missing file gives S9.4, never a usage text. | mod.rs:19-21 | usage_conflict_encode_decode, usage_bad_delimiter, flag_flatten_depth_too_big, usage_missing_file |

| S1.n | clause | provenance | cases |
|---|---|---|---|
| S1.150 | Every option is parsed and validated in both modes even when the chosen mode never reads it: `-d --delimiter x`, `-d --flatten-depth abc`, `-e --expand-paths maybe`, `--decode --expand-paths maybe` are argv errors (exit 2). | args.rs:32-58; mod.rs:20 | usage_bad_expand_paths, usage_ignored_flag_validated_decode, usage_ignored_flag_validated_encode |
| S1.151 | `--indent` is read by both modes. Encode: number of spaces per TOON nesting level (0 allowed: all lines start at column 1). Decode: the same number is both the indentation unit expected in the TOON input and the indent of the JSON output, where 0 selects the compact one-line JSON form [run: `-d --indent 0` on `a: 1\nb[2]: 1,2` → `{"a":1.0,"b":[1.0,2.0]}\n`; `-d --indent 4` on `a:\n    b: 1` → `{\n    "a": {\n        "b": 1.0\n    }\n}\n`]. The conversion rules themselves belong to the encode and decode parts. | mod.rs:35, 91 | flag_indent_0_encode, flag_indent_1_encode, flag_indent_16_encode, flag_indent_equals, flag_decode_indent_0, flag_decode_indent_4 |

| S1.154 | `--flatten-depth` without `--key-folding safe` changes nothing. | S1.155 | `--encode`/`--decode` always override the extension rule (S4.300): a `.toon` path with `--encode` is parsed as JSON, a `.json` path with `--decode` as TOON. | args.rs:87-94 | auto_toon_ext_forced_encode, auto_json_ext_forced_decode |


## S2. Inputs

### S2, part B (JSON text input and the two JSON writers): Inputs — JSON text (S2.1–S2.49) and conversion to the tool's value model (S2.50–S2.59)

| S2.n | clause | provenance | cases |
|---|---|---|---|
| S2.1 | In encode mode (how the mode is chosen is the S1 extractor's) the whole input, from stdin or from the input file, is read to its end before anything else happens and must be valid UTF-8 (S2.2). The byte sequence is then parsed as ONE JSON text by S2.3–S2.49. The parser works on bytes, never on characters or lines. Nothing is added, removed or translated before parsing: no BOM removal (S2.3), no newline translation, no trimming; a missing final newline and any amount of surrounding whitespace (S2.5) make no difference to the result. | `src/cli/mod.rs:29-31`, `src/cli/mod.rs:115-137`, `src/cli/conversion.rs:17-25`, `de.rs:2709-2714` | encstr_no_trailing_newline, encstr_whitespace_json |
| S2.2 | **UTF-8 gate.** The input is accepted only when every byte belongs to a well-formed UTF-8 sequence: `00–7F`; `C2–DF` + 1 continuation (`80–BF`); `E0` + `A0–BF` + cont.; `E1–EC` + 2 cont.; `ED` + `80–9F` + cont.; `EE–EF` + 2 cont.; `F0` + `90–BF` + 2 cont.; `F1–F3` + 3 cont.; `F4` + `80–8F` + 2 cont. Everything else is invalid: `C0`, `C1`, `F5–FF`, a lone continuation byte, overlong forms (`E0 80…`, `F0 80…`), UTF-8-encoded surrogates (`ED A0…`–`ED BF…`), values above U+10FFFF (`F4 90…`), and a sequence cut short by the end of input. The check covers the WHOLE input and happens before any JSON parsing, so an invalid byte that comes after a JSON syntax error still wins (`{bad json \xFF` reports the UTF-8 error). On failure nothing is parsed: stdout empty, exit 1, stderr is S9.75 (stdin) or S9.76 (file). Oracle runs: `"\xED\x9F\xBF"`, `"\xEE\x80\x80"`, `"\xF4\x8F\xBF\xBF"`, `"\xE0\xA0\x80"`, `"\xF0\x90\x80\x80"`, `"\xEF\xBF\xBF"` accepted; `"\xFF"`, `"\xC0\xAF"`, `"\xC1\xBF"`, `"\xC2"`, `"\xE2\x82"`, `"\x80"`, `"\xED\xA0\x80"`, `"\xED\xBF\xBF"`, `"\xF4\x90\x80\x80"`, `"\xF5\x80\x80\x80"`, `"\xE0\x80\x80"`, `"\xF0\x80\x80\x80"` rejected. | `src/cli/mod.rs:127-137` (Rust std `read_to_string`), `src/error.rs:24` | jsonerr_invalid_utf8_stdin, jsonerr_invalid_utf8_file, jsonerr_overlong_utf8, jsonerr_truncated_utf8 jsonerr_utf8_after_syntax_error, jsonerr_utf8_surrogate, jsonerr_utf8_above_max, jsonerr_overlong_utf8_3byte, jsonerr_overlong_utf8_4byte |
| S2.3 | A UTF-8 byte-order mark is NOT skipped. Input beginning `EF BB BF` fails with `expected value at line 1 column 1` (S9.58), because `EF` is not a byte that can start a value. | `de.rs:1393-1456` (no BOM branch anywhere in `de.rs`/`read.rs`) | jsonerr_bom_prefixed |
| S2.4 | **Top level.** A JSON text is: optional whitespace, exactly one value (S2.6), optional whitespace, end of input. Any other byte after the value is `trailing characters` (S9.67). A value of any kind is accepted at the root: object, array, string, number, `true`, `false`, `null`. Empty or whitespace-only input is `EOF while parsing a value` (S9.50). | `de.rs:2507-2518`, `de.rs:146-151` | encstr_whitespace_json, jsonerr_empty, jsonerr_whitespace, jsonerr_trailing_chars, jsonerr_trailing_after_obj, encnum_root |
| S2.5 | **Whitespace** is exactly four bytes: space 0x20, TAB 0x09, LF 0x0A, CR 0x0D. Any run of them (including none) is allowed before the root value, after it, after `[`, `{`, `,`, `:` and before `]`, `}`, `,`, `:`. It is not allowed inside a literal or a number, and inside a string a space is content while TAB, LF, CR are errors (S2.17). NO other byte or character is whitespace: VT 0x0B, FF 0x0C, NUL, U+0085, U+00A0, U+2028, U+2003, U+FEFF are ordinary offending bytes wherever they appear outside a string. There are no comments: `/` and `#` are offending bytes. Example accepted: ` ⏎⇥{ "a" : [ 1 , 2 ] ,␍⏎ "b" : { } }  ⏎⏎` encodes exactly like `{"a":[1,2],"b":{}}`. | `de.rs:255-266` | encstr_whitespace_json, jsonerr_form_feed_ws, jsonerr_nbsp_ws, jsonerr_comment jsonerr_vt_ws, jsonerr_ls_ws, encstr_ws_everywhere |
| S2.6 | **Value dispatch.** After whitespace, the first byte selects the value kind: `n` → literal `null`; `t` → literal `true`; `f` → literal `false` (S2.7); `-` or `0`–`9` → number (S2.8–S2.14); `"` → string (S2.15–S2.24); `[` → array (S2.25); `{` → object (S2.27). End of input here is `EOF while parsing a value` (S9.50). Any other byte is `expected value` (S9.58), reported at that byte. So `.5`, `+1`, `NaN`, `Infinity`, `TRUE`, `True`, `None`, `undefined`, `'a'`, `]`, `}`, `,`, `:` all give `expected value … column 1`; but `-Infinity` and `-NaN` give `invalid number … column 2` because the minus already selected the number rule (S10.27). | `de.rs:1393-1456` | jsonerr_dot_start, jsonerr_plus, jsonerr_nan, jsonerr_infinity, jsonerr_upper_true, jsonerr_single_quotes, jsonerr_missing_value, jsonerr_second_line_error, jsonerr_unicode_in_error_col jsonerr_minus_infinity |
| S2.7 | **Literals.** After the selecting byte, the following bytes must be exactly `ull`, `rue`, `alse` (lower-case ASCII), compared one byte at a time. The first differing byte is `expected ident` (S9.57) reported just after that byte; end of input before the literal is complete is `EOF while parsing a value` (S9.50). Nothing is required after a complete literal: what follows is judged by the enclosing context, so `nulll` and `truetrue` are `trailing characters` at column 5, and `[true false]` is ``expected `,` or `]` `` at column 7. | `de.rs:445-460`, `de.rs:1405-1419` | jsonerr_bad_true, jsonerr_bad_null, jsonerr_bad_ident, encstr_root_null, encstr_root_bool jsonerr_ident_newline, jsonerr_ident_multibyte, jsonerr_ident_then_more |
| S2.8 | **Number, lexical grammar** (exactly RFC 8259): optional `-`; integer part that is either the single digit `0` or a digit `1`–`9` followed by any number of digits; optional fraction: `.` followed by ONE OR MORE digits; optional exponent: `e` or `E`, an optional single `+` or `-`, then ONE OR MORE digits. Digits are ASCII `0`–`9` only. No leading `+`, no leading `.`, no hex, no `_`, no `Infinity`/`NaN`. There is no limit on the number of digits in any part (S2.13 for what overflow means). | `de.rs:462-507`, `de.rs:509-528`, `de.rs:530-565`, `de.rs:567-621`, `de.rs:717-738`, `de.rs:837-853` | encnum_ints, encnum_decimals, encnum_exponents, encnum_long_mantissa |
| S2.9 | Number: after `-` a digit is required. End of input → `EOF while parsing a value`; any other byte → `invalid number` reported just after that byte (`-x` 1:2, `-.5` 1:2, `--1` 1:2, `- 1` 1:2, `[-]` 1:3, `-⏎1` 2:0). | `de.rs:462-468`, `de.rs:505` | jsonerr_lone_minus, jsonerr_minus_word jsonerr_minus_newline |
| S2.10 | Number: an integer part that starts with `0` ends there. When the byte right after that `0` is another digit the error is `invalid number`, reported just after that second digit (`01` 1:2, `00` 1:2, `-01` 1:3, `[1,01]` 1:5, `0123456` 1:2). `0`, `-0`, `0.5`, `0e0`, `0E+5` are valid. | `de.rs:471-477` | jsonerr_leading_zero, encnum_root_negative_zero jsonerr_neg_leading_zero |
| S2.11 | Number: after `.` at least one digit is required. With no digit: end of input → `EOF while parsing a value` at `len`; any other byte → `invalid number` reported just after that byte (`1.x` 1:3, `1.e5` 1:3, `1. 5` 1:3, `[1.]` 1:4, `-0.e1` 1:4, `1.⏎` 2:0). | `de.rs:536-558` | jsonerr_dot_end jsonerr_dot_nondigit, jsonerr_dot_newline |
| S2.12 | Number: after `e`/`E` and the optional single sign at least one digit is required. End of input → `EOF while parsing a value`; any other byte (including a second sign) → `invalid number` reported just after that byte (`1ex` 1:3, `1e+x` 1:4, `1e 5` 1:3, `[1e]` 1:4, `[1e+]` 1:5, `1e++1` 1:4, `1.5e+-1` 1:6, `1e⏎5` 2:0). Exponent digits may have leading zeros (`1e+05` = 100000, `1e-05`). | `de.rs:573-600` | jsonerr_exp_end, encnum_exponents jsonerr_exp_sign_end, jsonerr_exp_nondigit, jsonerr_exp_double_sign |
| S2.13 | Number: the token is the LONGEST prefix that fits S2.8 (maximal munch) and ends before the first byte that cannot continue it; that byte is then judged by the enclosing context, never by the number rule. Hence at the root `1x`, `0x10`, `1_000`, `1,000` → `trailing characters` 1:2; `1.5.5`, `1e5e5`, `1e5.5` → `trailing characters` 1:4; inside an array `[1x]` → ``expected `,` or `]` `` 1:3; inside an object `{"a":1x}` → ``expected `,` or `}` ``. | `de.rs:499-501`, `de.rs:561-564`, `de.rs:602-620` | jsonerr_trailing_chars, jsonerr_missing_comma jsonerr_number_then_letter, jsonerr_hex_number |
| S2.14 | **Number out of range** (S9.61). Two triggers. (a) *Lexical:* while reading exponent digits the running exponent value (decimal value of the exponent digits read so far, leading zeros harmless) would exceed 2147483647. Then: when the exponent sign is `-`, or when every digit of the integer and fraction parts is `0`, the remaining exponent digits are consumed and the number is zero (it is NOT an error: `1e-99999999999999999999` → 0, `0e99999999999999999999` → 0, `-0e999…` → 0); otherwise the error is raised AT ONCE, reported just after the digit that caused the excess, not at the end of the token (`1e99999999999` is 13 bytes and reports 1:12; `1e+99999999999x` reports 1:13; `{"a":1e99999999999999999999}` reports 1:17). (b) *By value:* after the complete token has been consumed, X-C's conversion finds a non-zero digit string whose net decimal exponent is above 308 or whose product overflows binary64; the error is reported at the END of the token (`1e309` 1:5, `-1e309` 1:6, `1.8e308` 1:7, `1.7976931348623159e308` 1:22, `[1e400,2]` 1:6, `⏎ 1e999` 2:6, `{"é":1e999}` 1:11, 309 digits `9` → 1:309, `1` + 309 zeros + `.5` → 1:312). Underflow is never an error (`1e-400` → 0). `1e308`, `1.7976931348623157e308`, 308 digits `9` are accepted. Which values overflow is X-C's clause; only trigger (a) and both positions are owned here. | `de.rs:602-612`, `de.rs:859-874`, `de.rs:639-672` | encnum_overflow_positive, encnum_overflow_negative, encnum_overflow_huge_exponent, encnum_zero_huge_exponent, encnum_tiny_huge_exponent jsonerr_out_of_range_long_int, jsonerr_out_of_range_then_more, jsonerr_exponent_overflow_column_2147483648, jsonerr_exponent_overflow_column_2147483640 |
| S2.15 | **String.** Starts at `"` and ends at the next `"` that is not part of an escape. Between them, every byte `20–FF` other than `"` (0x22) and `\` (0x5C) is content, copied unchanged; because of S2.2 this means every Unicode scalar value from U+0020 up, including DEL U+007F, the C1 controls U+0080–U+009F, U+2028, U+2029, U+FEFF, U+FFFE, U+FFFF and all non-BMP characters, written raw. End of input before the closing quote is `EOF while parsing a string` (S9.51). There is no length limit. | `read.rs:494-538`, `read.rs:836-838` | encstr_unicode, encstr_escapes_in, jsonerr_unterminated_string encstr_raw_del_in |
| S2.16 | String content is not normalized, trimmed or re-encoded by the reader: the parsed string is the exact sequence of Unicode scalar values (raw ones as given, escaped ones per S2.18–S2.21). `"é"` (U+00E9) and `"é"` written as `e` + U+0301 are different strings. | `read.rs:513-524` | encstr_unicode encstr_duplicate_keys_escaped |
| S2.17 | **Raw control bytes** `00–1F` inside a string (TAB, LF, CR and NUL included) are an error: `control character (\u0000-\u001F) found while parsing a string` (S9.62), reported just after the control byte; when that byte is LF the report is therefore `line+1 column 0`. A string cannot span lines. | `read.rs:532-535`, `read.rs:836-838` | jsonerr_raw_newline_in_string, jsonerr_raw_tab_in_string jsonerr_raw_nul_in_string, jsonerr_raw_us_in_string, jsonerr_raw_cr_in_string |
| S2.18 | **Simple escapes.** `\` followed by one byte: `"` → U+0022, `\` → U+005C, `/` → U+002F, `b` → U+0008, `f` → U+000C, `n` → U+000A, `r` → U+000D, `t` → U+0009, `u` → S2.19. Escape letters are case-sensitive. Any other byte → `invalid escape` (S9.59) reported just after that byte (`"\x"` 1:3, `"\a"`, `"\0"`, `"\'"`, `"\U0041"` 1:3, `"ab\qcd"` 1:5, `"\⏎"` 2:0, `"\é"` 1:3 — inside the two-byte character). End of input right after the backslash → `EOF while parsing a string`. | `read.rs:874-895` | encstr_escapes_in, jsonerr_bad_escape encstr_escapes_in_all, jsonerr_escape_newline, jsonerr_escape_multibyte, jsonerr_escape_upper_u, jsonerr_eof_after_backslash |
| S2.19 | **`\u` escape, reading the four digits.** Let `h` be the offset of the byte after the `u`. When fewer than four bytes remain in the whole input (`len − h < 4`) the error is `EOF while parsing a string` reported at `len`, whatever those bytes are (`"\u12"` is 6 bytes: 3 bytes remain → 1:6). Otherwise the four bytes at `h … h+3` are taken BLINDLY (they may include the closing quote, an LF, or part of a multi-byte character) and each must be `0–9`, `A–F` or `a–f` (case-insensitive, mixed case allowed); when any is not, the error is `invalid escape` reported at `h+4`, after all four bytes (`"\u12zz"` 1:7, `"\uzzzz"` 1:7, `"\u123"` 1:7 — the quote was the fourth byte, `"\u12"⏎` 2:0, `"\u1⏎23"` 2:2, `"\u+123"` 1:7, `"\u12éx"` 1:7). The value `n` is the 16-bit number the four digits spell. | `read.rs:621-635`, `read.rs:1050-1089` | jsonerr_short_hex, jsonerr_bad_hex jsonerr_short_hex_newline, jsonerr_hex_multibyte |
| S2.20 | `\u` escape, `n` outside `D800–DFFF`: the scalar value U+`n`. `\u0000` is accepted and yields a real U+0000 inside the string (it then travels raw into the TOON output). `A` is `A`, `\` a backslash, `"` a quote (they do not end the string). | `read.rs:916-922` | encstr_escapes_in |
| S2.21 | `\u` escape, `n` in `D800–DBFF` (high surrogate): it MUST be followed immediately by a second escape `\u` + four hex digits whose value `n2` is in `DC00–DFFF`; the result is the single scalar `0x10000 + ((n − 0xD800) × 1024) + (n2 − 0xDC00)`. `😀` = `😀` = U+1F600; `𐀀` = U+10000; `􏿿` = U+10FFFF. | `read.rs:924-971` | encstr_escapes_in encstr_escapes_in_all |
| S2.22 | `\u` escape, `n` in `DC00–DFFF` as the FIRST escape (a lone low surrogate): `lone leading surrogate in hex escape` (S9.64) reported at `h+4` (`"\udc00"` 1:7, `"ab\udc00"` 1:9, `⏎"\udd1e"` 2:7, `"é\udc00"` 1:9). The message says "leading" although the surrogate is a trailing one (S10.23). | `read.rs:911-914` | jsonerr_lone_low_surrogate |
| S2.23 | High surrogate not followed by `\u`: with `h` as in S2.19, the byte at `h+4` must be `\` and the byte at `h+5` must be `u`. End of input at either place → `EOF while parsing a string` at `len` (`"\ud800` 1:7, `"\ud800\` 1:8). A different byte at `h+4` → `unexpected end of hex escape` (S9.65) reported at `h+5`, just after that byte (`"\ud800"` 1:8, `"\ud800x"` 1:8, `"\ud800é"` 1:8, `"\ud800⏎"` 2:0); a different byte at `h+5` → the same message reported at `h+6` (`"\ud800\n"` 1:9, `"\ud800\\"` 1:9, `"\ud800\⏎"` 2:0). | `read.rs:927-953` | jsonerr_lone_high_surrogate jsonerr_high_then_escape, jsonerr_high_then_newline, jsonerr_eof_after_high_surrogate, jsonerr_eof_after_high_surrogate_backslash |
| S2.24 | High surrogate followed by a second `\u` escape: its four digits are read by S2.19 with `h2 = h+6` (so: fewer than four bytes left → `EOF while parsing a string` at `len`, `"\ud800\udc0` 1:12; a non-hex byte → `invalid escape` at `h2+4`, `"\ud800\uzzzz"` 1:13, `"\ud800\udc0"` 1:13). | S2.25 | **Array.** `[`, whitespace, then either `]` (empty array) or a value; after each value, whitespace, then `]` (end) or `,` followed by whitespace and another value. Errors: end of input where `]`, `,` or the first value is awaited → `EOF while parsing a list` (S9.52); a byte other than `]`/`,` after a value → ``expected `,` or `]` `` (S9.55) at that byte; after a `,`: `]` → `trailing comma` (S9.66) at the `]`, end of input → `EOF while parsing a value` (NOT "a list"), anything else is judged as a value by S2.6 (so `[1,,2]` → `expected value` 1:4, `[1,}` → `expected value` 1:4). A `,` right after `[` is `expected value` (`[,]` 1:2). Element order is kept (S6.12).
